========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi. It's, uh, nice to meet you too. I guess I'm here because things have just been... really off lately, and it's getting to the point where other people are noticing, which is kind of embarrassing, honestly. Like, my supervisor at work—I do customer service, you know, on the phones all day—she actually pulled me aside because of how I was acting. I came in because people can actually see me either moving like I’m underwater, taking forever to answer a simple question or even just get out of a chair, or else I'm doing the exact opposite. Like, when I'm at home, I'll just be pacing my apartment back and forth, back and forth, and rubbing my hands together like I’m trying to shake off a bad call, you know? It's just this weird... physical thing. Sometimes it feels like every movement takes a monumental amount of effort, like gravity got turned up or something, and I just sit there staring at my headset. And then other times, I can't sit still at all, and I'm just wearing a path in the carpet, wringing my hands, feeling this nervous energy but not really doing anything productive with it. It's just really frustrating because it's not like I'm trying to be slow or trying to be restless, it's just happening to my body, and everybody can see it.

No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's not like I'm totally frozen or anything, you know? Like, I can still talk, it just takes... well, it takes a lot out of me. It's more like my brain is wading through molasses, and by the time I figure out what I want to say, the moment has almost passed. But I'm never completely mute. Like, if my supervisor asks me a direct question about a customer's account, I'll answer her. It's just that the whole process of forming the thought, moving my mouth, getting the air out... it feels like such a heavy lift. I've never had a moment where I physically couldn't get a single word out, even if I really, really wanted to. It's just the speed and the energy behind it that's all messed up. Elevated? Like, really happy or energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just... normal, let alone elevated or high-energy. It's funny you ask that, actually, because my mom was asking me something kind of similar the other day when she called. She was like, "Are you ever in a good mood anymore?" And I really had to think about it, and the answer is just... no. It's always this heavy, dragging feeling. I never get those upward swings. 

I mean, sure, I get annoyed at work sometimes. Like when a customer is screaming at me over the phone about a late fee that they clearly caused themselves because they didn't read the billing statement—which happens, I swear, like five times a day, and it's always the exact same script with them. But that's just normal customer service frustration, you know? It's not like I'm flying off the handle or snapping at people for no reason, or feeling this intense, out-of-control irritability. If anything, I'm just too tired to even get properly mad anymore. I just kind of take it, read my apology script, and let them vent because it's easier than actually drumming up the energy to feel angry. 

And as for feeling unusually good or on top of the world? Yeah, right. I wish. It's more like I'm just constantly sinking. Even when I try to watch a comedy or something on Netflix that used to make me laugh, I just sit there staring at the screen. I don't get that spark at all. So yeah, definitely no periods where I felt super high or energetic or anything like that. It's pretty much just been this constant, flat, exhausting low.

I've never had a moment where I physically couldn't get a single word out, even if I really, really wanted to. It's just the speed and the energy behind it that's all messed up. I can always force something out if I absolutely have to, it just feels exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a distinct period when your mood was unusually elevated or irritable compared with your usual self?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Like, really happy or energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just... normal, let alone elevated or high-energy. It's funny you ask that, actually, because my mom was asking me something kind of similar the other day when she called. She was like, \"Are you ever in a good mood anymore?\" And I really had to think about it, and the answer is just... no. It's always this heavy, dragging feeling. I never get those upward swings. \n\nI mean, sure, I get annoyed at work sometimes. Like when a customer is screaming at me over the phone about a late fee that they clearly caused themselves because they didn't read the billing statement—which happens, I swear, like five times a day, and it's always the exact same script with them. But that's just normal customer service frustration, you know? It's not like I'm flying off the handle or snapping at people for no reason, or feeling this intense, out-of-control irritability. If anything, I'm just too tired to even get properly mad anymore. I just kind of take it, read my apology script, and let them vent because it's easier than actually drumming up the energy to feel angry. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually good or on top of the world? Yeah, right. I wish. It's more like I'm just constantly sinking. Even when I try to watch a comedy or something on Netflix that used to make me laugh, I just sit there staring at the screen. I don't get that spark at all. So yeah, definitely no periods where I felt super high or energetic or anything like that. It's pretty much just been this constant, flat, exhausting low.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, holding my body in an unusual position... I guess it depends on what you mean by "unusual," right? Because I don't think I'm doing anything super bizarre, like twisting myself into a pretzel or standing on one leg for an hour or anything dramatic like you might see in a movie. But... well, there are these moments, especially when things just feel really, really overwhelming. Like, if I've just logged off from a particularly brutal shift where every caller was angry and my brain just feels like it's buzzing and completely drained at the same time. 

I'll go into the kitchen, right? Just to get some coffee or maybe a glass of water, and I'll realize... I don't know how long I've been standing there, but I'll catch myself just frozen. Like, I'll literally have my coffee mug halfway up to my mouth, and I'll just be stopped there, staring at the cabinet door or the tile on the backsplash. And it's not like I'm paralyzed, I just... I forget to finish the movement, I guess? It’s like the signal from my brain to my hand just completely short-circuits because there's too much other noise going on in my head. I'll just stand there with my arm raised for I don't even know how long, a minute, maybe a few minutes, until I sort of snap out of it and realize how weird I must look. 

And then there's this other thing I do. It’s not exactly standing still, but it's just as pointless, I think. I'll get into this weird loop in the living room. I have this little pathway between the couch and the TV stand, and I'll just start walking it. Not pacing like I'm anxious—like I mentioned before with the hand-wringing—but just... walking this exact same little circle or oval shape, over and over again, for absolutely no reason. I'm not going anywhere, I'm not looking for anything, I'm just doing this repetitive loop. My neighbor downstairs actually banged on the ceiling with a broom handle once because I guess I was doing it in my socks for so long that the thudding was driving him crazy. And that’s what snapped me out of it. I just stopped and thought, "What am I even doing?" It’s just like my body gets stuck on a track or paused like a video, and I don't even realize it's happening until something breaks the spell. So, I don't know if that counts as an unusual position, but it definitely feels like my body just isn't taking directions properly anymore.

Like, when I'm at home, I'll just be pacing my apartment back and forth, back and forth, and rubbing my hands together like I’m trying to shake off a bad call, you know? It's just this weird... physical thing. Sometimes it feels like every movement takes a monumental amount of effort, like gravity got turned up or something, and I just sit there staring at my headset. And then other times, I can't sit still at all, and I'm just wearing a path in the carpet, wringing my hands, feeling this nervous energy but not really doing anything productive with it. It's just really frustrating because it's not like I'm trying to be slow or trying to be restless, it's just happening to my body, and everybody can see it.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you feel slowed down, have you ever been unable to speak or respond even though you wanted to?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's not like I'm totally frozen or anything, you know? Like, I can still talk, it just takes... well, it takes a lot out of me. It's more like my brain is wading through molasses, and by the time I figure out what I want to say, the moment has almost passed. But I'm never completely mute. Like, if my supervisor asks me a direct question about a customer's account, I'll answer her. It might take me a few extra seconds, and I might sound really flat or quiet, and I know she looks at me like she's waiting for the rest of the sentence, but the words do come out eventually. It's just that the whole process of forming the thought, moving my mouth, getting the air out... it feels like such a heavy lift. I've never had a moment where I physically couldn't get a single word out, even if I really, really wanted to. It's just the speed and the energy behind it that's all messed up. I can always force something out if I absolutely have to, it just feels exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a distinct period when your mood was unusually elevated or irritable compared with your usual self?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Like, really happy or energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just... normal, let alone elevated or high-energy. It's funny you ask that, actually, because my mom was asking me something kind of similar the other day when she called. She was like, \"Are you ever in a good mood anymore?\" And I really had to think about it, and the answer is just... no. It's always this heavy, dragging feeling. I never get those upward swings. \n\nI mean, sure, I get annoyed at work sometimes. Like when a customer is screaming at me over the phone about a late fee that they clearly caused themselves because they didn't read the billing statement—which happens, I swear, like five times a day, and it's always the exact same script with them. But that's just normal customer service frustration, you know? It's not like I'm flying off the handle or snapping at people for no reason, or feeling this intense, out-of-control irritability. If anything, I'm just too tired to even get properly mad anymore. I just kind of take it, read my apology script, and let them vent because it's easier than actually drumming up the energy to feel angry. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually good or on top of the world? Yeah, right. I wish. It's more like I'm just constantly sinking. Even when I try to watch a comedy or something on Netflix that used to make me laugh, I just sit there staring at the screen. I don't get that spark at all. So yeah, definitely no periods where I felt super high or energetic or anything like that. It's pretty much just been this constant, flat, exhausting low.\n\nDoctor: During the times you feel slowed down, have you ever held your body in an unusual position for a long time without moving?\n\nPatient: Well, holding my body in an unusual position... I guess it depends on what you mean by \"unusual,\" right? Because I don't think I'm doing anything super bizarre, like twisting myself into a pretzel or standing on one leg for an hour or anything dramatic like you might see in a movie. But... well, there are these moments, especially when things just feel really, really overwhelming. Like, if I've just logged off from a particularly brutal shift where every caller was angry and my brain just feels like it's buzzing and completely drained at the same time. \n\nI'll go into the kitchen, right? Just to get some coffee or maybe a glass of water, and I'll realize... I don't know how long I've been standing there, but I'll catch myself just frozen. Like, I'll literally have my coffee mug halfway up to my mouth, and I'll just be stopped there, staring at the cabinet door or the tile on the backsplash. And it's not like I'm paralyzed, I just... I forget to finish the movement, I guess? It’s like the signal from my brain to my hand just completely short-circuits because there's too much other noise going on in my head. I'll just stand there with my arm raised for I don't even know how long, a minute, maybe a few minutes, until I sort of snap out of it and realize how weird I must look. \n\nAnd then there's this other thing I do. It’s not exactly standing still, but it's just as pointless, I think. I'll get into this weird loop in the living room. I have this little pathway between the couch and the TV stand, and I'll just start walking it. Not pacing like I'm anxious—like I mentioned before with the hand-wringing—but just... walking this exact same little circle or oval shape, over and over again, for absolutely no reason. I'm not going anywhere, I'm not looking for anything, I'm just doing this repetitive loop. My neighbor downstairs actually banged on the ceiling with a broom handle once because I guess I was doing it in my socks for so long that the thudding was driving him crazy. And that’s what snapped me out of it. I just stopped and thought, \"What am I even doing?\" It’s just like my body gets stuck on a track or paused like a video, and I don't even realize it's happening until something breaks the spell. So, I don't know if that counts as an unusual position, but it definitely feels like my body just isn't taking directions properly anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I guess it depends on what you mean by \"unusual,\" right? But... well, there are these moments, especially when things just feel really, really overwhelming. Like, if I've just logged off from a particularly brutal shift where every caller was angry and my brain just feels like it's buzzing and completely drained at the same time. \n\nI'll go into the kitchen, right? Just to get some coffee or maybe a glass of water, and I'll realize... I don't know how long I've been standing there, but I'll catch myself just frozen. Like, I'll literally have my coffee mug halfway up to my mouth, and I'll just be stopped there, staring at the cabinet door or the tile on the backsplash. And it's not like I'm paralyzed, I just... I forget to finish the movement, I guess? It’s like the signal from my brain to my hand just completely short-circuits because there's too much other noise going on in my head. I'll just stand there with my arm raised for I don't even know how long, a minute, maybe a few minutes, until I sort of snap out of it and realize how weird I must look. \n\nAnd then there's this other thing I do. It’s not exactly standing still, but it's just as pointless, I think. I'll get into this weird loop in the living room. I have this little pathway between the couch and the TV stand, and I'll just start walking it. Not pacing like I'm anxious—like I mentioned before with the hand-wringing—but just... walking this exact same little circle or oval shape, over and over again, for absolutely no reason. I'm not going anywhere, I'm not looking for anything, I'm just doing this repetitive loop. My neighbor downstairs actually banged on the ceiling with a broom handle once because I guess I was doing it in my socks for so long that the thudding was driving him crazy. And that’s what snapped me out of it. I just stopped and thought, \"What am I even doing?\" It’s just like my body gets stuck on a track or paused like a video, and I don't even realize it's happening until something breaks the spell. So, I don't know if that counts as an unusual position, but it definitely feels like my body just isn't taking directions properly anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you are frozen in place or walking the same loop, can someone get your attention and have you respond normally?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / customer_service_representative / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"When I get overwhelmed, I sometimes catch myself standing in the kitchen with my coffee mug halfway to my mouth or walking the same little loop by the couch for no real reason.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, they definitely can. Like I was saying about my neighbor banging on the ceiling with his broom—that loud thud completely broke the spell. It’s not like I’m in a coma or trapped in my own body and unable to hear anything. If there’s a loud noise, or if someone actually talks to me, I snap right out of it. 

Like, there was this one time last week, right after a really brutal shift where I just got yelled at by callers for four hours straight. I was just so overwhelmed, and I caught myself doing that exact thing I just told you about—standing in the kitchen with my coffee mug just hovering halfway to my mouth, totally frozen. And right then, my cell phone started ringing. I keep the volume all the way up because I'm terrified of missing a message from my supervisor, so it’s this really jarring, loud ringtone. And the second it went off, it was like someone snapped their fingers right in front of my face. I just blinked, realized my arm was getting tired, put the mug down on the counter, and picked up the phone. 

It was my mom, actually, and I just answered it like normal. "Hey Mom, what's up?" She didn't have any idea that I had just been standing there like a statue for who knows how long. We had a totally normal, boring conversation about her garden. I didn't sound confused or anything. 

So, yeah, if someone gets my attention, I can respond completely normally. I don't talk weird or act out of it. I mean, I might sound a little exhausted, but that's just how I always sound these days anyway. It’s just that if nothing interrupts me... if the phone doesn't ring or the neighbor doesn't get mad, I'll just keep doing it. I'll just keep walking that same little loop by the couch for no real reason, just pacing that exact oval over and over, or standing by the cabinets. I just need something from the outside world to pull me out of it, and once it does, I’m totally present again. It's embarrassing to think about, honestly, but yeah, I can definitely respond if someone reaches out.

was getting things done, but I still heard the supervisor voice or felt sure the old ticketing software was flagging me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I can still keep a roof over my head, but I’ve lost momentum with steady work because I miss deadlines, avoid calls, and come across as unreliable. I also see friends less and let basic routines like meals, errands, and hiking slide unless I force myself.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 2 years and 3 months, starting after I was fired from my customer-support job.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and tracking beliefs have been around for about a year and 8 months, not just during the worst sadness.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 2 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The depression side has been present for about 2 years and 1 month, with only brief periods where it eases up.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / customer_service_representative / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S028\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had a distinct period when your mood was unusually elevated or irritable compared with your usual self?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Like, really happy or energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just... normal, let alone elevated or high-energy. It's funny you ask that, actually, because my mom was asking me something kind of similar the other day when she called. She was like, \"Are you ever in a good mood anymore?\" And I really had to think about it, and the answer is just... no. It's always this heavy, dragging feeling. I never get those upward swings. \n\nI mean, sure, I get annoyed at work sometimes. Like when a customer is screaming at me over the phone about a late fee that they clearly caused themselves because they didn't read the billing statement—which happens, I swear, like five times a day, and it's always the exact same script with them. But that's just normal customer service frustration, you know? It's not like I'm flying off the handle or snapping at people for no reason, or feeling this intense, out-of-control irritability. If anything, I'm just too tired to even get properly mad anymore. I just kind of take it, read my apology script, and let them vent because it's easier than actually drumming up the energy to feel angry. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually good or on top of the world? Yeah, right. I wish. It's more like I'm just constantly sinking. Even when I try to watch a comedy or something on Netflix that used to make me laugh, I just sit there staring at the screen. I don't get that spark at all. So yeah, definitely no periods where I felt super high or energetic or anything like that. It's pretty much just been this constant, flat, exhausting low.\n\nDoctor: During the times you feel slowed down, have you ever held your body in an unusual position for a long time without moving?\n\nPatient: Well, holding my body in an unusual position... I guess it depends on what you mean by \"unusual,\" right? Because I don't think I'm doing anything super bizarre, like twisting myself into a pretzel or standing on one leg for an hour or anything dramatic like you might see in a movie. But... well, there are these moments, especially when things just feel really, really overwhelming. Like, if I've just logged off from a particularly brutal shift where every caller was angry and my brain just feels like it's buzzing and completely drained at the same time. \n\nI'll go into the kitchen, right? Just to get some coffee or maybe a glass of water, and I'll realize... I don't know how long I've been standing there, but I'll catch myself just frozen. Like, I'll literally have my coffee mug halfway up to my mouth, and I'll just be stopped there, staring at the cabinet door or the tile on the backsplash. And it's not like I'm paralyzed, I just... I forget to finish the movement, I guess? It’s like the signal from my brain to my hand just completely short-circuits because there's too much other noise going on in my head. I'll just stand there with my arm raised for I don't even know how long, a minute, maybe a few minutes, until I sort of snap out of it and realize how weird I must look. \n\nAnd then there's this other thing I do. It’s not exactly standing still, but it's just as pointless, I think. I'll get into this weird loop in the living room. I have this little pathway between the couch and the TV stand, and I'll just start walking it. Not pacing like I'm anxious—like I mentioned before with the hand-wringing—but just... walking this exact same little circle or oval shape, over and over again, for absolutely no reason. I'm not going anywhere, I'm not looking for anything, I'm just doing this repetitive loop. My neighbor downstairs actually banged on the ceiling with a broom handle once because I guess I was doing it in my socks for so long that the thudding was driving him crazy. And that’s what snapped me out of it. I just stopped and thought, \"What am I even doing?\" It’s just like my body gets stuck on a track or paused like a video, and I don't even realize it's happening until something breaks the spell. So, I don't know if that counts as an unusual position, but it definitely feels like my body just isn't taking directions properly anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen in place or walking the same loop, can someone get your attention and have you respond normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they definitely can. Like I was saying about my neighbor banging on the ceiling with his broom—that loud thud completely broke the spell. It’s not like I’m in a coma or trapped in my own body and unable to hear anything. If there’s a loud noise, or if someone actually talks to me, I snap right out of it. \n\nLike, there was this one time last week, right after a really brutal shift where I just got yelled at by callers for four hours straight. I was just so overwhelmed, and I caught myself doing that exact thing I just told you about—standing in the kitchen with my coffee mug just hovering halfway to my mouth, totally frozen. And right then, my cell phone started ringing. I keep the volume all the way up because I'm terrified of missing a message from my supervisor, so it’s this really jarring, loud ringtone. And the second it went off, it was like someone snapped their fingers right in front of my face. I just blinked, realized my arm was getting tired, put the mug down on the counter, and picked up the phone. \n\nIt was my mom, actually, and I just answered it like normal. \"Hey Mom, what's up?\" She didn't have any idea that I had just been standing there like a statue for who knows how long. We had a totally normal, boring conversation about her garden. I didn't sound confused or anything. \n\nSo, yeah, if someone gets my attention, I can respond completely normally. I don't talk weird or act out of it. I mean, I might sound a little exhausted, but that's just how I always sound these days anyway. It’s just that if nothing interrupts me... if the phone doesn't ring or the neighbor doesn't get mad, I'll just keep doing it. I'll just keep walking that same little loop by the couch for no real reason, just pacing that exact oval over and over, or standing by the cabinets. I just need something from the outside world to pull me out of it, and once it does, I’m totally present again. It's embarrassing to think about, honestly, but yeah, I can definitely respond if someone reaches out.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you recently been diagnosed with a new medical or neurological condition?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

No, no, I haven't. Honestly, I haven't even been to a regular doctor in... wow, I don't even know how long. Definitely not recently. With my schedule at the call center, it's just so incredibly hard to find the time, you know? You're chained to the headset taking back-to-back calls all day, and the absolute last thing you want to do on your one day off is sit in a cold waiting room filling out endless clipboards of paperwork and reading magazines from three years ago. Plus, the health insurance I get through my job is... well, it's not the greatest, with the deductible and everything, so I really try to avoid going unless something is physically broken or I'm dying. 

So, no, no new medical conditions, and definitely nothing neurological. I mean, my head feels foggy all the time, and I do get these really killer tension headaches by the end of my shift from staring at the dual monitors for eight hours straight and tensing up my shoulders while people yell at me. But that's just normal stress and eye strain, right? That's not an actual medical condition. My mom actually keeps getting on my case about it, telling me I should go get a full physical, do all the bloodwork, check my vitamins. She’s convinced I might have a thyroid thing or a vitamin D deficiency because I pretty much never go outside in the sun anymore—which, fair enough, I really don't. But I haven't actually gone in to get tested for any of that. It just feels like trying to climb a mountain just to drum up the energy to make the phone call to set up the appointment, let alone actually driving there. 

So, yeah, long story short, no doctor has told me I have anything new going on physically or neurologically. I haven't given them the chance to, really. It's just the same old exhaustion.

It's embarrassing to think about, honestly, but yeah, I can definitely respond if someone reaches out.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently been diagnosed with a new medical or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. Honestly, I haven't even been to a regular doctor in... wow, I don't even know how long. Definitely not recently. With my schedule at the call center, it's just so incredibly hard to find the time, you know? You're chained to the headset taking back-to-back calls all day, and the absolute last thing you want to do on your one day off is sit in a cold waiting room filling out endless clipboards of paperwork and reading magazines from three years ago. Plus, the health insurance I get through my job is... well, it's not the greatest, with the deductible and everything, so I really try to avoid going unless something is physically broken or I'm dying. \n\nSo, no, no new medical conditions, and definitely nothing neurological. I mean, my head feels foggy all the time, and I do get these really killer tension headaches by the end of my shift from staring at the dual monitors for eight hours straight and tensing up my shoulders while people yell at me. But that's just normal stress and eye strain, right? That's not an actual medical condition. My mom actually keeps getting on my case about it, telling me I should go get a full physical, do all the bloodwork, check my vitamins. She’s convinced I might have a thyroid thing or a vitamin D deficiency because I pretty much never go outside in the sun anymore—which, fair enough, I really don't. But I haven't actually gone in to get tested for any of that. It just feels like trying to climb a mountain just to drum up the energy to make the phone call to set up the appointment, let alone actually driving there. \n\nSo, yeah, long story short, no doctor has told me I have anything new going on physically or neurologically. I haven't given them the chance to, really. It's just the same old exhaustion.\n\n[Last Question]\n{\"intent\":\"Assess for catalepsy, a feature that would support catatonia over depression or anxiety\",\"question\":\"When you are frozen, if someone gently moves one of your arms or legs into a different position, does it stay there?\"\"}\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / customer_service_representative / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I don't really know, honestly. Because, like I was saying, I live by myself, you know? It's just me in that apartment. So when I get stuck standing there with the coffee mug halfway up, or when I'm pacing that little oval by the TV, there's literally no one else around to even see it happen, let alone come up and try to move me. I mean, my mom calls, obviously, but she lives all the way across town and she doesn't really come over much these days. And honestly, my place is a bit of a disaster area right now anyway—just stacks of mail I haven't opened and laundry I haven't had the energy to fold since I don't even know when—so I definitely don't invite friends or coworkers over after my shifts. 

So, yeah, nobody has ever been there to try moving my arm or leg. And, I mean, if you think about it, even if someone *were* there, that would be a pretty weird thing for them to do, right? Just walking up and repositioning my arm like I'm some kind of mannequin in a mall window? I feel like if someone actually walked up and physically touched me, or grabbed my arm to move it, just the feeling of them touching me would probably snap me right out of the loop anyway. Just like the loud ringtone does, or the neighbor hitting the ceiling. Because I'm not unconscious, you know? I'm still awake. So I'd probably just blink and look at them and ask what on earth they were doing touching me. 

But yeah, since it's only ever just me, completely alone in the kitchen or the living room, I really have no idea if my arm would stay put or not. It's just never come up because I'm always by myself when it happens.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it’s a little hard to say, honestly, because I feel like I’ve just been in this heavy, gray funk for so long that I don't even really know what a "normal" mood feels like anymore. It's just my baseline now. But to answer your question... no, it’s not just when I’m feeling actively, terribly depressed. It happens even when I’m just... existing, you know? Like on a random Tuesday afternoon when nothing particularly awful has happened and I'm just feeling completely numb. 

Actually, to be completely honest with you, these physical things—the freezing up and the pacing—that’s a huge part of why I even agreed to come in and talk to someone in the first place. Because it’s gotten to the point where it's not just me noticing it when I'm alone. Other people can actually see it happening now, which is just incredibly mortifying. 

Like, my mom, or even my supervisor on our weekly video check-ins... they’ve pointed out that sometimes I’m moving like I’m completely underwater. Just incredibly sluggish, like the air in my apartment is made of molasses or something. I’ll be sitting there, and it takes me forever to just answer a simple question. There's this huge delay. Or I'll just sit there and it takes me an eternity to finally get out of a chair. It’s like my brain sends the signal to my legs to stand up, and the message just gets lost on the way down, so I just sit there staring. 

And then, on the complete flip side of being stuck in the chair, there's the pacing. I'll just be pacing my apartment and obsessively rubbing my hands together, over and over. It's exactly like... well, you know when you get a really horrible, screaming caller at work, and they're just completely tearing into you, and after you hang up you just feel that gross, jittery adrenaline? It's like I’m trying to physically shake off a bad call, just rubbing my hands and pacing a hole in the floor. But I'll be doing it for absolutely no reason. I haven't even taken a call, and there I am, rubbing my hands raw. 

So, yeah, it definitely happens whether I'm feeling actively depressed or just having a regular, dull day. It’s so frustrating because it's like I can’t control the speed of my own body anymore, and now other people are actually seeing it happen, which just makes me want to hide even more.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F41.9, F06.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi. It's, uh, nice to meet you too. I guess I'm here because things have just been... really off lately, and it's getting to the point where other people are noticing, which is kind of embarrassing, honestly. Like, my supervisor at work—I do customer service, you know, on the phones all day—she actually pulled me aside because of how I was acting. I came in because people can actually see me either moving like I’m underwater, taking forever to answer a simple question or even just get out of a chair, or else I'm doing the exact opposite. Like, when I'm at home, I'll just be pacing my apartment back and forth, back and forth, and rubbing my hands together like I’m trying to shake off a bad call, you know? It's just this weird... physical thing. Sometimes it feels like every movement takes a monumental amount of effort, like gravity got turned up or something, and I just sit there staring at my headset. And then other times, I can't sit still at all, and I'm just wearing a path in the carpet, wringing my hands, feeling this nervous energy but not really doing anything productive with it. It's just really frustrating because it's not like I'm trying to be slow or trying to be restless, it's just happening to my body, and everybody can see it.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you feel slowed down, have you ever been unable to speak or respond even though you wanted to?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's not like I'm totally frozen or anything, you know? Like, I can still talk, it just takes... well, it takes a lot out of me. It's more like my brain is wading through molasses, and by the time I figure out what I want to say, the moment has almost passed. But I'm never completely mute. Like, if my supervisor asks me a direct question about a customer's account, I'll answer her. It might take me a few extra seconds, and I might sound really flat or quiet, and I know she looks at me like she's waiting for the rest of the sentence, but the words do come out eventually. It's just that the whole process of forming the thought, moving my mouth, getting the air out... it feels like such a heavy lift. I've never had a moment where I physically couldn't get a single word out, even if I really, really wanted to. It's just the speed and the energy behind it that's all messed up. I can always force something out if I absolutely have to, it just feels exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a distinct period when your mood was unusually elevated or irritable compared with your usual self?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Like, really happy or energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just... normal, let alone elevated or high-energy. It's funny you ask that, actually, because my mom was asking me something kind of similar the other day when she called. She was like, \"Are you ever in a good mood anymore?\" And I really had to think about it, and the answer is just... no. It's always this heavy, dragging feeling. I never get those upward swings. \n\nI mean, sure, I get annoyed at work sometimes. Like when a customer is screaming at me over the phone about a late fee that they clearly caused themselves because they didn't read the billing statement—which happens, I swear, like five times a day, and it's always the exact same script with them. But that's just normal customer service frustration, you know? It's not like I'm flying off the handle or snapping at people for no reason, or feeling this intense, out-of-control irritability. If anything, I'm just too tired to even get properly mad anymore. I just kind of take it, read my apology script, and let them vent because it's easier than actually drumming up the energy to feel angry. \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually good or on top of the world? Yeah, right. I wish. It's more like I'm just constantly sinking. Even when I try to watch a comedy or something on Netflix that used to make me laugh, I just sit there staring at the screen. I don't get that spark at all. So yeah, definitely no periods where I felt super high or energetic or anything like that. It's pretty much just been this constant, flat, exhausting low.\n\nDoctor: During the times you feel slowed down, have you ever held your body in an unusual position for a long time without moving?\n\nPatient: Well, holding my body in an unusual position... I guess it depends on what you mean by \"unusual,\" right? Because I don't think I'm doing anything super bizarre, like twisting myself into a pretzel or standing on one leg for an hour or anything dramatic like you might see in a movie. But... well, there are these moments, especially when things just feel really, really overwhelming. Like, if I've just logged off from a particularly brutal shift where every caller was angry and my brain just feels like it's buzzing and completely drained at the same time. \n\nI'll go into the kitchen, right? Just to get some coffee or maybe a glass of water, and I'll realize... I don't know how long I've been standing there, but I'll catch myself just frozen. Like, I'll literally have my coffee mug halfway up to my mouth, and I'll just be stopped there, staring at the cabinet door or the tile on the backsplash. And it's not like I'm paralyzed, I just... I forget to finish the movement, I guess? It’s like the signal from my brain to my hand just completely short-circuits because there's too much other noise going on in my head. I'll just stand there with my arm raised for I don't even know how long, a minute, maybe a few minutes, until I sort of snap out of it and realize how weird I must look. \n\nAnd then there's this other thing I do. It’s not exactly standing still, but it's just as pointless, I think. I'll get into this weird loop in the living room. I have this little pathway between the couch and the TV stand, and I'll just start walking it. Not pacing like I'm anxious—like I mentioned before with the hand-wringing—but just... walking this exact same little circle or oval shape, over and over again, for absolutely no reason. I'm not going anywhere, I'm not looking for anything, I'm just doing this repetitive loop. My neighbor downstairs actually banged on the ceiling with a broom handle once because I guess I was doing it in my socks for so long that the thudding was driving him crazy. And that’s what snapped me out of it. I just stopped and thought, \"What am I even doing?\" It’s just like my body gets stuck on a track or paused like a video, and I don't even realize it's happening until something breaks the spell. So, I don't know if that counts as an unusual position, but it definitely feels like my body just isn't taking directions properly anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen in place or walking the same loop, can someone get your attention and have you respond normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they definitely can. Like I was saying about my neighbor banging on the ceiling with his broom—that loud thud completely broke the spell. It’s not like I’m in a coma or trapped in my own body and unable to hear anything. If there’s a loud noise, or if someone actually talks to me, I snap right out of it. \n\nLike, there was this one time last week, right after a really brutal shift where I just got yelled at by callers for four hours straight. I was just so overwhelmed, and I caught myself doing that exact thing I just told you about—standing in the kitchen with my coffee mug just hovering halfway to my mouth, totally frozen. And right then, my cell phone started ringing. I keep the volume all the way up because I'm terrified of missing a message from my supervisor, so it’s this really jarring, loud ringtone. And the second it went off, it was like someone snapped their fingers right in front of my face. I just blinked, realized my arm was getting tired, put the mug down on the counter, and picked up the phone. \n\nIt was my mom, actually, and I just answered it like normal. \"Hey Mom, what's up?\" She didn't have any idea that I had just been standing there like a statue for who knows how long. We had a totally normal, boring conversation about her garden. I didn't sound confused or anything. \n\nSo, yeah, if someone gets my attention, I can respond completely normally. I don't talk weird or act out of it. I mean, I might sound a little exhausted, but that's just how I always sound these days anyway. It’s just that if nothing interrupts me... if the phone doesn't ring or the neighbor doesn't get mad, I'll just keep doing it. I'll just keep walking that same little loop by the couch for no real reason, just pacing that exact oval over and over, or standing by the cabinets. I just need something from the outside world to pull me out of it, and once it does, I’m totally present again. It's embarrassing to think about, honestly, but yeah, I can definitely respond if someone reaches out.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently been diagnosed with a new medical or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. Honestly, I haven't even been to a regular doctor in... wow, I don't even know how long. Definitely not recently. With my schedule at the call center, it's just so incredibly hard to find the time, you know? You're chained to the headset taking back-to-back calls all day, and the absolute last thing you want to do on your one day off is sit in a cold waiting room filling out endless clipboards of paperwork and reading magazines from three years ago. Plus, the health insurance I get through my job is... well, it's not the greatest, with the deductible and everything, so I really try to avoid going unless something is physically broken or I'm dying. \n\nSo, no, no new medical conditions, and definitely nothing neurological. I mean, my head feels foggy all the time, and I do get these really killer tension headaches by the end of my shift from staring at the dual monitors for eight hours straight and tensing up my shoulders while people yell at me. But that's just normal stress and eye strain, right? That's not an actual medical condition. My mom actually keeps getting on my case about it, telling me I should go get a full physical, do all the bloodwork, check my vitamins. She’s convinced I might have a thyroid thing or a vitamin D deficiency because I pretty much never go outside in the sun anymore—which, fair enough, I really don't. But I haven't actually gone in to get tested for any of that. It just feels like trying to climb a mountain just to drum up the energy to make the phone call to set up the appointment, let alone actually driving there. \n\nSo, yeah, long story short, no doctor has told me I have anything new going on physically or neurologically. I haven't given them the chance to, really. It's just the same old exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\":\"Assess for catalepsy, a feature that would support catatonia over depression or anxiety\",\"question\":\"When you are frozen, if someone gently moves one of your arms or legs into a different position, does it stay there?\"\"}\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't really know, honestly. Because, like I was saying, I live by myself, you know? It's just me in that apartment. So when I get stuck standing there with the coffee mug halfway up, or when I'm pacing that little oval by the TV, there's literally no one else around to even see it happen, let alone come up and try to move me. I mean, my mom calls, obviously, but she lives all the way across town and she doesn't really come over much these days. And honestly, my place is a bit of a disaster area right now anyway—just stacks of mail I haven't opened and laundry I haven't had the energy to fold since I don't even know when—so I definitely don't invite friends or coworkers over after my shifts. \n\nSo, yeah, nobody has ever been there to try moving my arm or leg. Just walking up and repositioning my arm like I'm some kind of mannequin in a mall window? Just like the loud ringtone does, or the neighbor hitting the ceiling. Because I'm not unconscious, you know? I'm still awake. It's just never come up because I'm always by myself when it happens.\n\nDoctor: When these periods of freezing or repetitive movement happen, are they only present when you feel depressed, or do they also occur when your mood is otherwise normal?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s a little hard to say, honestly, because I feel like I’ve just been in this heavy, gray funk for so long that I don't even really know what a \"normal\" mood feels like anymore. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting there—well, not sitting, pacing, obviously—but it's not like I'm walking back and forth because I'm terrified of something. I know what it feels like to be really worried, you know? Like if I'm worried about making rent, or if I'm dreading logging into the phone system because I know the queue is backed up and people are going to be screaming at me the second I connect. When I feel *that* kind of worry, my stomach gets all tied up in knots and my chest gets super tight. 

But this... this pacing and hand rubbing thing? It's completely different. It's almost like my brain is totally disconnected from what my body is doing. I'll just be walking that same little path—it's from the edge of the kitchen counter, past the coffee table, right to the window, and then back again. Over and over. I've done it so much I swear I'm going to wear a trench into the cheap carpet my landlord put in. But my mind isn't racing with fearful thoughts or anything like that while I'm doing it. It's more just... blank? Or foggy, like I said before. 

I'm just rubbing my hands together because there's this weird, gross, jittery energy trapped in my arms and legs, and I just have to move. But there's no actual thought attached to it. No fear, no panic about the future, no worrying about anything specific. It's just my body doing its own weird thing while I'm just kind of checked out, if that makes sense. So no, I wouldn't call it fear at all. Just... physically restless, I guess, without the actual anxiety part.

And honestly, my place is a bit of a disaster area right now anyway—just stacks of mail I haven't opened and laundry I haven't had the energy to fold since I don't even know when—so I definitely don't invite friends or coworkers over after my shifts. \n\nSo, yeah, nobody has ever been there to try moving my arm or leg. And, I mean, if you think about it, even if someone *were* there, that would be a pretty weird thing for them to do, right? Just walking up and repositioning my arm like I'm some kind of mannequin in a mall window? I feel like if someone actually walked up and physically touched me, or grabbed my arm to move it, just the feeling of them touching me would probably snap me right out of the loop anyway. Just like the loud ringtone does, or the neighbor hitting the ceiling. Because I'm not unconscious, you know? I'm still awake. So I'd probably just blink and look at them and ask what on earth they were doing touching me. \n\nBut yeah, since it's only ever just me, completely alone in the kitchen or the living room, I really have no idea if my arm would stay put or not. It's just never come up because I'm always by myself when it happens.\n\nDoctor: When these periods of freezing or repetitive movement happen, are they only present when you feel depressed, or do they also occur when your mood is otherwise normal?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s a little hard to say, honestly, because I feel like I’ve just been in this heavy, gray funk for so long that I don't even really know what a \"normal\" mood feels like anymore. It's just my baseline now. But to answer your question... no, it’s not just when I’m feeling actively, terribly depressed. It happens even when I’m just... existing, you know? Like on a random Tuesday afternoon when nothing particularly awful has happened and I'm just feeling completely numb. \n\nActually, to be completely honest with you, these physical things—the freezing up and the pacing—that’s a huge part of why I even agreed to come in and talk to someone in the first place. Because it’s gotten to the point where it's not just me noticing it when I'm alone. Other people can actually see it happening now, which is just incredibly mortifying. \n\nLike, my mom, or even my supervisor on our weekly video check-ins... they’ve pointed out that sometimes I’m moving like I’m completely underwater. Just incredibly sluggish, like the air in my apartment is made of molasses or something. I’ll be sitting there, and it takes me forever to just answer a simple question. There's this huge delay. Or I'll just sit there and it takes me an eternity to finally get out of a chair. It’s like my brain sends the signal to my legs to stand up, and the message just gets lost on the way down, so I just sit there staring. \n\nAnd then, on the complete flip side of being stuck in the chair, there's the pacing. I'll just be pacing my apartment and obsessively rubbing my hands together, over and over. It's exactly like... well, you know when you get a really horrible, screaming caller at work, and they're just completely tearing into you, and after you hang up you just feel that gross, jittery adrenaline? It's like I’m trying to physically shake off a bad call, just rubbing my hands and pacing a hole in the floor. But I'll be doing it for absolutely no reason. I haven't even taken a call, and there I am, rubbing my hands raw. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely happens whether I'm feeling actively depressed or just having a regular, dull day. It’s so frustrating because it's like I can’t control the speed of my own body anymore, and now other people are actually seeing it happen, which just makes me want to hide even more.\n\nDoctor: When you pace and rub your hands, are you feeling intense fear or excessive worry at that moment?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting there—well, not sitting, pacing, obviously—but it's not like I'm walking back and forth because I'm terrified of something. I know what it feels like to be really worried, you know? Like if I'm worried about making rent, or if I'm dreading logging into the phone system because I know the queue is backed up and people are going to be screaming at me the second I connect. When I feel *that* kind of worry, my stomach gets all tied up in knots and my chest gets super tight. \n\nBut this... this pacing and hand rubbing thing? It's completely different. It's almost like my brain is totally disconnected from what my body is doing. I'll just be walking that same little path—it's from the edge of the kitchen counter, past the coffee table, right to the window, and then back again. Over and over. I've done it so much I swear I'm going to wear a trench into the cheap carpet my landlord put in. But my mind isn't racing with fearful thoughts or anything like that while I'm doing it. It's more just... blank? Or foggy, like I said before. \n\nI'm just rubbing my hands together because there's this weird, gross, jittery energy trapped in my arms and legs, and I just have to move. But there's no actual thought attached to it. No fear, no panic about the future, no worrying about anything specific. It's just my body doing its own weird thing while I'm just kind of checked out, if that makes sense. So no, I wouldn't call it fear at all. Just... physically restless, I guess, without the actual anxiety part.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, have you felt depressed or lost interest in most activities nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean, like I was saying earlier about that heavy, gray funk... it's definitely been there every single day for the past couple of weeks. Probably longer, honestly, but definitely the last two weeks. But the interest part... that's almost worse than just feeling down, if I'm being completely honest. Because I used to have things that really, you know, grounded me. Things that made me feel like *me*, outside of just being the guy who gets yelled at about billing errors on the phone all day. 

I used to be super into sketching, for one. I have these really nice, thick-paper Moleskine notebooks and a whole set of different graphite pencils that I used to keep right on my coffee table. And I’d also keep this really detailed journal of coffee notes—like, I’d buy different roasts from local cafes and write down the flavor profiles, the exact brewing temperatures, the ratio of water to beans, all that nerdy stuff. It was just a really nice, quiet morning ritual for me. And then on the weekends, I was always out hiking. There’s this whole trail system about forty minutes from my apartment, and I'd go out there every Saturday, just to get away from the screens and the noise. 

Those things—the sketching, the coffee notes, and the weekend hikes—they really used to make me feel like myself. But lately... it’s just completely gone. Like the desire to do any of it has just totally evaporated. I’ll actually try to force myself to do it, you know? I’ll sit down at my little desk and open up the notebook, or I’ll get my hiking boots out of the closet and physically sit on the edge of the bed and lace them up, thinking that just going through the motions will kickstart something in my brain. But I just sit there looking at the blank page, or staring down at my boots, and I just feel... nothing. There's just nothing pulling me toward it anymore. 

It’s like the cord got unplugged. I used to get this little spark of excitement when I found a new trail on the map, or when I perfectly dialed in a pour-over, but now it just feels pointless. Actually, it doesn't even feel pointless, because that implies I have a feeling about it at all. It just feels like a total void. And then I just end up untying the boots, putting them back in the closet, and going back to pacing around the living room or just sitting in that chair for hours. It’s really unsettling when the things that used to define your downtime just stop working, you know? It's like, who even am I if I don't care about the things I actually enjoy?

Just incredibly sluggish, like the air in my apartment is made of molasses or something. I’ll be sitting there, and it takes me forever to just answer a simple question. There's this huge delay. Or I'll just sit there and it takes me an eternity to finally get out of a chair. It’s like my brain sends the signal to my legs to stand up, and the message just gets lost on the way down, so I just sit there staring. \n\nAnd then, on the complete flip side of being stuck in the chair, there's the pacing. Like if I'm worried about making rent, or if I'm dreading logging into the phone system because I know the queue is backed up and people are going to be screaming at me the second I connect. Well, yeah, actually. It’s... it’s really hard to talk about, but since you’re asking specifically, yes. But it really only happens when I hit those absolute rock-bottom stretches. You know, those worst low periods where the heavy funk I was talking about turns into something just completely crushing. The times when I’m barely eating anything—like, maybe I’ll have a handful of dry cereal out of the box over the course of an entire day because chewing an actual meal feels like climbing a mountain—and I’m barely sleeping, just lying there staring at the ceiling fan for hours, feeling this immense, suffocating guilt about every little mistake I’ve ever made in my life. 

When I get into that specific state, that's when things get really... weird. Like, I’ll start hearing the headset beeps. If you've never worked in a call center, there's this very specific, high-pitched double beep that plays directly into your ear right before a caller connects. It's designed to jolt your attention so you're ready to read the script. But during those really bad weeks, I’ll hear that exact double beep clear as day when I’m not even logged in. I’ll be standing in my kitchen at two in the morning, or I'll be in the shower, and suddenly—*beep-beep*. It’s not just a ringing in my ears, it sounds like I’m wearing the headset and the system is forcing a call through to me. I’ll actually physically flinch and brace myself for a customer to start yelling, even though I'm alone in my apartment. 

And then, along with the beeps... you asked about believing things that others say aren't true. My mom and my buddy from work have both told me this is impossible, but when I'm in that really dark, guilty, exhausted place, I get completely stuck on this idea that my old job’s system is still tracking me. Not my current job, but the one I had a couple of years ago. They were notorious for micromanaging—they tracked our "idle time" down to the exact second. If you were away from your desk for more than four minutes to use the restroom, it flagged your metrics. 

When things get really bad for me, I become absolutely convinced that their tracking software is somehow still monitoring me. Even though I don't even own the same laptop anymore, I'll get this overwhelming, terrifying certainty that my old manager is sitting at his dashboard watching a timer tick up while I'm just sitting on my couch. I'll start feeling incredibly guilty and panicked just for getting up to get a glass of water, because I'm thinking, "They're logging this as unauthorized away time, they're going to write me up, they're watching my status change to yellow." I know, logically, right now sitting here with you, that I don't work for them anymore. But when I'm in that deep hole, I can't shake it. The beeps keep happening, and I just feel this heavy, paranoid weight that the old system is still recording every single move I make in my own home.

It's just that the whole process of forming the thought, moving my mouth, getting the air out... it feels like such a heavy lift. She was like, \"Are you ever in a good mood anymore?\" And I really had to think about it, and the answer is just... no. It's always this heavy, dragging feeling. Not pacing like I'm anxious—like I mentioned before with the hand-wringing—but just... walking this exact same little circle or oval shape, over and over again, for absolutely no reason. I'm not going anywhere, I'm not looking for anything, I'm just doing this repetitive loop. My neighbor downstairs actually banged on the ceiling with a broom handle once because I guess I was doing it in my socks for so long that the thudding was driving him crazy. And that’s what snapped me out of it. I just stopped and thought, \"What am I even doing?\" It’s just like my body gets stuck on a track or paused like a video, and I don't even realize it's happening until something breaks the spell. So, I don't know if that counts as an unusual position, but it definitely feels like my body just isn't taking directions properly anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen in place or walking the same loop, can someone get your attention and have you respond normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they definitely can. Like I was saying about my neighbor banging on the ceiling with his broom—that loud thud completely broke the spell. It’s not like I’m in a coma or trapped in my own body and unable to hear anything. If there’s a loud noise, or if someone actually talks to me, I snap right out of it. \n\nLike, there was this one time last week, right after a really brutal shift where I just got yelled at by callers for four hours straight. I was just so overwhelmed, and I caught myself doing that exact thing I just told you about—standing in the kitchen with my coffee mug just hovering halfway to my mouth, totally frozen. And right then, my cell phone started ringing. I keep the volume all the way up because I'm terrified of missing a message from my supervisor, so it’s this really jarring, loud ringtone. And the second it went off, it was like someone snapped their fingers right in front of my face. I just blinked, realized my arm was getting tired, put the mug down on the counter, and picked up the phone. \n\nIt was my mom, actually, and I just answered it like normal. \"Hey Mom, what's up?\" She didn't have any idea that I had just been standing there like a statue for who knows how long. We had a totally normal, boring conversation about her garden. I didn't sound confused or anything. \n\nSo, yeah, if someone gets my attention, I can respond completely normally. I don't talk weird or act out of it. I mean, I might sound a little exhausted, but that's just how I always sound these days anyway. It’s just that if nothing interrupts me... if the phone doesn't ring or the neighbor doesn't get mad, I'll just keep doing it. I'll just keep walking that same little loop by the couch for no real reason, just pacing that exact oval over and over, or standing by the cabinets. I just need something from the outside world to pull me out of it, and once it does, I’m totally present again. It's embarrassing to think about, honestly, but yeah, I can definitely respond if someone reaches out.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently been diagnosed with a new medical or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't. Honestly, I haven't even been to a regular doctor in... wow, I don't even know how long. Definitely not recently. With my schedule at the call center, it's just so incredibly hard to find the time, you know? You're chained to the headset taking back-to-back calls all day, and the absolute last thing you want to do on your one day off is sit in a cold waiting room filling out endless clipboards of paperwork and reading magazines from three years ago. Plus, the health insurance I get through my job is... well, it's not the greatest, with the deductible and everything, so I really try to avoid going unless something is physically broken or I'm dying. \n\nSo, no, no new medical conditions, and definitely nothing neurological. I mean, my head feels foggy all the time, and I do get these really killer tension headaches by the end of my shift from staring at the dual monitors for eight hours straight and tensing up my shoulders while people yell at me. But that's just normal stress and eye strain, right? That's not an actual medical condition. My mom actually keeps getting on my case about it, telling me I should go get a full physical, do all the bloodwork, check my vitamins. She’s convinced I might have a thyroid thing or a vitamin D deficiency because I pretty much never go outside in the sun anymore—which, fair enough, I really don't. But I haven't actually gone in to get tested for any of that. It just feels like trying to climb a mountain just to drum up the energy to make the phone call to set up the appointment, let alone actually driving there. \n\nSo, yeah, long story short, no doctor has told me I have anything new going on physically or neurologically. I haven't given them the chance to, really. I don't really know, honestly. Because, like I was saying, I live by myself, you know? It's just me in that apartment. So when I get stuck standing there with the coffee mug halfway up, or when I'm pacing that little oval by the TV, there's literally no one else around to even see it happen, let alone come up and try to move me. I mean, my mom calls, obviously, but she lives all the way across town and she doesn't really come over much these days. And honestly, my place is a bit of a disaster area right now anyway—just stacks of mail I haven't opened and laundry I haven't had the energy to fold since I don't even know when—so I definitely don't invite friends or coworkers over after my shifts. \n\nSo, yeah, nobody has ever been there to try moving my arm or leg. And, I mean, if you think about it, even if someone *were* there, that would be a pretty weird thing for them to do, right? Just walking up and repositioning my arm like I'm some kind of mannequin in a mall window? I feel like if someone actually walked up and physically touched me, or grabbed my arm to move it, just the feeling of them touching me would probably snap me right out of the loop anyway. Just like the loud ringtone does, or the neighbor hitting the ceiling. Because I'm not unconscious, you know? I'm still awake. So I'd probably just blink and look at them and ask what on earth they were doing touching me. \n\nBut yeah, since it's only ever just me, completely alone in the kitchen or the living room, I really have no idea if my arm would stay put or not. It's just never come up because I'm always by myself when it happens.\n\nDoctor: When these periods of freezing or repetitive movement happen, are they only present when you feel depressed, or do they also occur when your mood is otherwise normal?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s a little hard to say, honestly, because I feel like I’ve just been in this heavy, gray funk for so long that I don't even really know what a \"normal\" mood feels like anymore. It's just my baseline now. But to answer your question... no, it’s not just when I’m feeling actively, terribly depressed. It happens even when I’m just... existing, you know? Like on a random Tuesday afternoon when nothing particularly awful has happened and I'm just feeling completely numb. \n\nActually, to be completely honest with you, these physical things—the freezing up and the pacing—that’s a huge part of why I even agreed to come in and talk to someone in the first place. Because it’s gotten to the point where it's not just me noticing it when I'm alone. Other people can actually see it happening now, which is just incredibly mortifying. \n\nLike, my mom, or even my supervisor on our weekly video check-ins... they’ve pointed out that sometimes I’m moving like I’m completely underwater. Just incredibly sluggish, like the air in my apartment is made of molasses or something. I’ll be sitting there, and it takes me forever to just answer a simple question. There's this huge delay. Or I'll just sit there and it takes me an eternity to finally get out of a chair. It’s like my brain sends the signal to my legs to stand up, and the message just gets lost on the way down, so I just sit there staring. \n\nAnd then, on the complete flip side of being stuck in the chair, there's the pacing. I'll just be pacing my apartment and obsessively rubbing my hands together, over and over. It's exactly like... well, you know when you get a really horrible, screaming caller at work, and they're just completely tearing into you, and after you hang up you just feel that gross, jittery adrenaline? It's like I’m trying to physically shake off a bad call, just rubbing my hands and pacing a hole in the floor. But I'll be doing it for absolutely no reason. I haven't even taken a call, and there I am, rubbing my hands raw. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely happens whether I'm feeling actively depressed or just having a regular, dull day.
